Kerr-Newman-(A)dS background.

To achieve this goal, we consider a solution of the linearized Einstein-Maxwell equations, possibly coupled to matter, on a Kerr-Newman-(A)dS background of mass and charge parameters
m_{1}
and
q_{1}
, vanishing for
r\leq r_{1}
and coinciding with the linearized Kerr-Newman-(A)dS solution of mass and charge parameters
m_{2}=m_{1}+\Delta m
and
q_{2}=q_{1}+\Delta q
for
r\geq r_{2}
(and the same spin parameter
a
); if the energy computed from the linearized energy-momentum tensor with respect to the Killing vector field
K^{\alpha}
(which is now defined on the fixed Kerr-Newman-(A)dS background of mass and charge parameters
m_{1}
and
q_{1}
) is
\Delta M=\Delta m/\Xi^{2}
then
K^{\alpha}
does indeed give the correct definition of energy. Note that one such linearized solution, albeit for unphysical matter, can be obtained by linearizing the spacetime constructed in the previous sections; as we have shown, the Killing vector field
K^{\alpha}
does give the correct energy in this case. A simple application of the divergence theorem then shows that
K^{\alpha}
will give the same energy for any other linearized solution, including solutions corresponding to physical matter fields. Indeed, if
\delta g_{\alpha\beta}(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
is an arbitrary linearized metric,
\delta g_{\alpha\beta}^{0}(r,\theta)
is the linearization of the metric constructed in the previous subsections, and
\rho(t)
is a smooth function satisfying
\rho(t)\equiv 1
for
t\leq 0
and
\rho(t)\equiv 0
for
t\geq 1
, consider the linearized metric
\rho(t-t_{0})\delta g_{\alpha\beta}+(1-\rho(t-t_{0}))\delta g_{\alpha\beta}^{0}
. The linearized energy-momentum tensor corresponding to this metric has zero divergence in the Kerr-Newman-(A)dS background, coincides with the energy-momentum tensor of the arbitrary linearized metric for
t=t_{0}
, and with the energy-momentum tensor of
\delta g_{\alpha\beta}^{0}
for
t=t_{0}+1
. Moreover, it vanishes for
r\leq r_{1}
and it is time-independent for
r\geq r_{2}
(so in particular does not depend on the choice of
\delta g_{\alpha\beta}
in those regions). Applying the divergence theorem to the vector field
J^{\alpha}=\big(T^{\alpha\beta}+T_{\text{EM}}^{\alpha\beta}\big)K_{\beta}
in the hollow cylinder defined by
r_{1}\leq r\leq r_{2}
and
t_{0}\leq t\leq t_{0}+1
(see Fig.
24
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{r_{1}<r<r_{2}}\left(T^{\mu\nu}+T_{\text{EM}}^{\mu\nu}\right)K_{\mu}N_{\nu}dV_{3}-\int_{r_{1}<r<r_{2}}\left(T_{0}^{\mu\nu}+T_{\text{EM},0}^{\mu\nu}\right)K_{\mu}N_{\nu}dV_{3}

\displaystyle-\int_{r=r_{1}}\left(T^{\mu\nu}+T_{\text{EM}}^{\mu\nu}\right)K_{\mu}N_{\nu}dV_{3}+\int_{r=r_{2}}\left(T^{\mu\nu}+T_{\text{EM}}^{\mu\nu}\right)K_{\mu}N_{\nu}dV_{3}=0\,,

(511)

where the unit normal
N_{\alpha}
is future-pointing when timelike and outward-pointing when spacelike, and the energy-momentum tensor
T_{0}^{\alpha\beta}+T_{\text{EM},0}^{\alpha\beta}
refers to
\delta g_{\alpha\beta}^{0}
. Since the last two integrals do not depend on the choice of
\delta g_{\alpha\beta}
, and their sum clearly vanishes when one chooses
\delta g_{\alpha\beta}=\delta g_{\alpha\beta}^{0}
(because the first two integrals cancel in that case), it always vanishes; therefore we obtain

\int_{r_{1}<r<r_{2}}\left(T^{\mu\nu}+T_{\text{EM}}^{\mu\nu}\right)K_{\mu}N_{\nu}dV_{3}=\int_{r_{1}<r<r_{2}}\left(T_{0}^{\mu\nu}+T_{\text{EM},0}^{\mu\nu}\right)K_{\mu}N_{\nu}dV_{3}\,,

(512)

showing tha
K^{\alpha}
does indeed yield the correct energy for any linearized solution.

Figure 24:
Domain for the application of the divergence theorem.

29.4
Uniqueness of
K^{\alpha}

We have now identified a timelike Killing vector field
K^{\alpha}
in the Kerr-Newman-(A)dS spacetime, given by Eqs. (
471
) and (
472
), such that the definitions in Eqs. (
470
) and (
488
) give the correct total energy
\widetilde{E}
contained in linearized (test) fields. This energy is related to the variation
\Delta M=M_{2}-M_{1}
of the physical mass by Eqs. (
478
) and (
29.2
). Similarly, the definitions in Eqs. (
479
) and (
500
) give the correct total angular momentum
\widetilde{L}
in the test fields, which is related to the variation
\Delta L=a\Delta M
of the angular momentum by Eqs. (
484
) and (
29.2
). However, because the variations of energy and angular momentum are related through the spin parameter
a
, which we did not vary, the possibility that
K^{\alpha}
is not unique remains.

To understand this, we note that any other future-pointing timelike Killing vector field can be written in the form

\tilde{K}^{\alpha}=\gamma\left(K^{\alpha}+\epsilon Y^{\alpha}\right)\,,

(513)

with
\gamma>0
and
\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}
appropriately chosen. Combining Eqs. (
29.2
) and (
29.2
), we see that
\tilde{K}^{\alpha}
will also give the correct total energy
\widetilde{E}
contained in the unphysical field if and only if

\gamma\Delta M-\gamma\epsilon\Delta L=\Delta M\Leftrightarrow\gamma(1-\epsilon a)=1\,,

(514)

that is, if and only if

\tilde{K}^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{1-a\epsilon(a)}\left[K^{\alpha}+\epsilon(a)Y^{\alpha}\right]\,,

(515)

where we made it explicit that
\epsilon
is an unknown function of
a
. To show that
\epsilon(a)
must be identically zero, and therefore that
K^{\alpha}
is unique, we allow the spin parameter
a
to become a function of
r
in the region
r_{1}\leq r\leq r_{2}
, while keeping the mass and charge parameters fixed. To perform the linearization, we assume that
a(r)=a_{0}+\delta a(r)
varies infinitesimally between
a(r_{1})=a_{0}
and
a(r_{2})=a_{0}+\Delta a
(i. e.,
|\delta a(r)|\ll a_{0}
). Since in this case the calculations are much more involved than in the previous subsections, we assume that all quantities are analytic functions of
a
and expand them as power series of
a_{0}
(and to linear order in
\delta a(r)
). In particular, we have

\epsilon(a)=\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\epsilon_{n}a^{n}\,.

(516)

In what follows we will show that
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{1}=0
. Due to the complexity of the calculations, we have not computed the higher order coefficients
\epsilon_{n}
with
n\geq 2
, but we expect them to also vanish.

To further simplify calculations we consider only the Kerr-(A)dS case
q_{1}=q_{2}=0
. Using the definition of Eq. (
470
) with the Killing vector field
\tilde{K}(a_{0})
, and applying the same procedure of the previous subsections, we obtain the radial integral

\widetilde{E}=\int_{\mathcal{S}}T^{\mu\nu}(\tilde{K}(a_{0}))_{\mu}N_{\nu}dV_{3}=\int_{r_{1}}^{r_{2}}dr[\mathcal{G}(r)\delta a^{\prime}(r)+\mathcal{H}(r)\delta a^{\prime\prime}(r)]\,,

(517)

with the radial functions

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{15l^{4}r^{3}}\left[\left(10\epsilon_{0}l^{4}mr^{3}\mp 10\epsilon_{0}l^{2}r^{6}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad+a_{0}\Big(10\epsilon_{0}^{2}l^{4}mr^{3}\mp 10\epsilon_{0}^{2}l^{2}r^{6}+10\epsilon_{1}l^{4}mr^{3}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad\mp 10\epsilon_{1}l^{2}r^{6}+20l^{4}m^{2}+10l^{4}mr\pm 75l^{2}mr^{3}\mp 40l^{2}r^{4}+40r^{6}\Big)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad+a_{0}^{2}\Big(20\epsilon_{0}\epsilon_{1}l^{4}mr^{3}\mp 20\epsilon_{0}\epsilon_{1}l^{2}r^{6}+4\epsilon_{0}l^{4}m^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad+2\epsilon_{0}l^{4}mr\mp 5\epsilon_{0}l^{2}mr^{3}\mp 50\epsilon_{0}l^{2}r^{4}+52\epsilon_{0}r^{6}\Big)+\mathcal{O}(a_{0}^{3})\right]\,,

(518)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{30l^{4}r^{2}}\left[\left(10\epsilon_{0}l^{4}mr^{3}\pm 5\epsilon_{0}l^{2}r^{6}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad+a_{0}\Big(10\epsilon_{0}^{2}l^{4}mr^{3}\pm 5\epsilon_{0}^{2}l^{2}r^{6}+10\epsilon_{1}l^{4}mr^{3}\pm 5\epsilon_{1}l^{2}r^{6}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad+20l^{4}m^{2}+20l^{4}mr-20l^{4}r^{2}\mp 30l^{2}mr^{3}\pm 40l^{2}r^{4}-20r^{6}\Big)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad+a_{0}^{2}\Big(20\epsilon_{0}\epsilon_{1}l^{4}mr^{3}\pm 10\epsilon_{0}\epsilon_{1}l^{2}r^{6}+4\epsilon_{0}l^{4}m^{2}+4\epsilon_{0}l^{4}mr\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad-20\epsilon_{0}l^{4}r^{2}\mp 50\epsilon_{0}l^{2}mr^{3}\pm 50\epsilon_{0}l^{2}r^{4}-26\epsilon_{0}r^{6}\right)+\mathcal{O}(a_{0}^{3})\Big]\,.

(519)

Integrating Eq. (
517
) by parts, we obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}

\displaystyle=\int_{r_{1}}^{r_{2}}dr\left(\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}-\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\right)\delta a+\left[\left(\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right)\delta a+\mathcal{H}\delta a^{\prime}\right]_{r_{1}}^{r_{2}}\,.

(520)

Using
\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}(r)=\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(r)
,
\delta a^{\prime}(r_{2})=\delta a^{\prime}(r_{1})=0
, and

\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=-\epsilon_{0}m-a_{0}m\left(\epsilon_{0}^{2}+\epsilon_{1}\pm\frac{4}{l^{2}}\right)-2a_{0}^{2}\epsilon_{0}m\left(\epsilon_{1}\mp\frac{1}{l^{2}}\right)+\mathcal{O}(a_{0}^{3})\,,

we get

\widetilde{E}=-\left[\epsilon_{0}m+a_{0}m\left(\epsilon_{0}^{2}+\epsilon_{1}\pm\frac{4}{l^{2}}\right)+2a_{0}^{2}\epsilon_{0}m\left(\epsilon_{1}\mp\frac{1}{l^{2}}\right)\right]\Delta a+\mathcal{O}(a_{0}^{3})\,.

(521)

On the other hand, it is easily seen from (
431
) that

\Delta M=\mp\frac{4a_{0}m}{l^{2}}\Delta a+\mathcal{O}(a_{0}^{3})\,.

(522)

Finally, imposing
\widetilde{E}=\Delta M
as an equality of power series in
a_{0}
we obtain
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{1}=0
.

29.5
Extension of the main result

In the previous subsections we have shown that the timelike Killing vector field
K^{\alpha}
given by Eq. (
472
) is the correct choice to compute the energy of a test field in a Kerr-Newman-de Sitter background, at least in what concerns its interaction with the
BH
. On the other hand, it is well known that the null generator of the event horizon is
Z^{\alpha}=K^{\alpha}+\Omega Y^{\alpha}
, where
\Omega
is the thermodynamic angular velocity, that is, the angular velocity that occurs in the first law (see for instance
[
294
,
295
]
). Therefore, we can apply the main result of the previous section to conclude that test fields cannot destroy extremal Kerr-Newman-dS
BH
.
54
54

54

The statement of that result requires
K^{\alpha}
to be asymptotically timelike; however, it is clear from the proof that all that is in fact needed is that
K^{\alpha}
determines the correct notion of energy.

30
Discussion

In this chapter we proved that extremal Kerr-Newman or Kerr-Newman-AdS
BH
cannot be destroyed by interacting with (possibly charged) test fields satisfying the null energy condition at the event horizon and appropriate boundary conditions at infinity. This includes as particular cases all previous results of this kind obtained for scalar and electromagnetic test fields
[
251
,
252
,
253
,
254
]
. The corresponding results for test particles
[
248
,
249
,
250
]
can also be considered particular cases, since particles can be seen as singular limits of continuous media
[
272
,
273
]
. It is interesting to note that if the null energy condition is not satisfied then the weak cosmic censorship conjecture may indeed be violated, as shown in Refs.
[
296
,
297
]
for Dirac fields.

We have also shown that the timelike Killing vector field
K^{\alpha}
given by Eq. (
472
) gives the correct definition of energy for test fields propagating in the Kerr-Newman-dS spacetime. Additionally, we have confirmed that the timelike Killing vector field
K^{\alpha}
given by Eq. (
471
) gives the correct definition of energy for test fields propagating in the Kerr-Newman-AdS spacetime. Finally, we used this definition of energy to extend our main result to extremal Kerr-Newman-dS
BH
.
The technique employed in the last subsection, namely allowing parameters in the metric to become functions in order to interpolate between
BH
spacetimes with different physical masses, can be useful in other situations where the choice of the timelike Killing vector field with which to compute the energy of test fields is not clear. It is also possible that these ideas may play a role in determining an appropriate definition of mass for asymptotically de Sitter spacetimes.

Part III   Appendix

†
†
margin:

10

PN expansion of Einstein-Klein-Gordon

In this appendix, we show that the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system reduces to the Schrödinger-Poisson system in the Newtonian limit. Then, we obtain the equations describing a perturbation to the Newtonian fields up to first post-Newtonian corrections. Finally, we consider perturbations caused by a point particle. In this section we follow the treatment in Chapter 8.2 of Ref.
[
72
]
.

The Einstein-Klein-Gordon system is the set of field equations for
\Phi
and
g_{\mu\nu}
which is obtained through the variation of action (
2
) with respect to
\Phi^{*}
and
g_{\mu\nu}
, and reads

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\mu}\left(\sqrt{-g}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\Phi\right)=\mu_{S}^{2}c^{2}\Phi\,,

(523)

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}=\frac{8\pi}{c^{4}}\widetilde{T}_{\mu\nu}^{S}\,,

(524)

where the Einstein equations are written in an alternative form using the trace-reversed stress-energy tensor of the scalar field

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}^{S}_{\mu\nu}\equiv T^{S}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}T^{S}g_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{(\mu}\Phi^{*}\partial_{\nu)}\Phi+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\mu_{S}^{2}c^{2}|\Phi|^{2}\,.

In the last equations we used
\mathcal{U}_{S}\sim\mu_{S}^{2}c^{2}|\Phi|^{2}/2
, since we want to consider a (Newtonian) diluted scalar field
|\Phi|\ll 1
.
Moreover, in the Newtonian limit, we consider the spacetime metric ansatz

\displaystyle g_{00}

\displaystyle=-1-\frac{2}{c^{2}}U+\mathcal{O}(c^{-4})\,,

(525)

\displaystyle g_{0j}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(c^{-3})\,,\quad g_{jk}=\mathcal{O}(c^{-2})\,,

(526)

with
x^{0}=ct
and the Cartesian coordinates
x^{j}=\{x,y,z\}
. This gives the Ricci tensor components

\displaystyle R_{00}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c^{2}}\nabla^{2}U+\mathcal{O}(c^{-4})\,,

(527)

\displaystyle R_{0j}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(c^{-3})\,,\quad R_{jk}=\mathcal{O}(c^{-2})\,.

(528)

The non-relativistic limit of the scalar field
\Phi
is incorporated in our perturbation scheme by considering that
55
55

55

It corresponds to the assertion that, in the non-relativistic limit, the energy-momentum relation is
E\sim\hbar\mu_{S}c^{2}+\frac{1}{2\hbar\mu_{S}}p^{2}+\hbar\mu_{S}U
, with
p^{2}\ll(\hbar\mu_{S}c)^{2}
and
|U|\ll 1
. Here, the order parameter
\epsilon
is
\epsilon\sim\mathcal{O}(p/(\hbar\mu_{S}c))\sim\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{U}/c)
.

\displaystyle c^{-1}|\partial_{j}\phi|/|\phi|\sim\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\,,\quad c^{-2}|\partial_{t}\phi|/|\phi|\sim\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\,,

(529)

where we introduced an auxiliary scalar field
\phi
such that

\displaystyle\Phi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu_{S}}}e^{-i\mu_{S}c^{2}t}\phi\,.

(530)

Then, the components of the trace-reversed stress-energy tensor of the scalar field are

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{00}^{S}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mu_{S}c^{2}|\phi|^{2}+\mathcal{O}(c^{0})\,,

(531)

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{0j}^{S}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(c)\,,\quad\widetilde{T}_{jk}^{S}=\mathcal{O}(c^{2})\,.

(532)

Therefore, at Newtonian order, the Einstein equations reduce to the Poisson equation

\nabla^{2}U=4\pi\mu_{S}|\phi|^{2}\,,

(533)

which implies that
|\phi|\sim\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
.
On the other hand, it is easy to show that, at leading order in
\epsilon
(and in the Newtonian limit), the Klein-Gordon equation reduces to the Schrödinger equation

i\partial_{t}\phi=-\frac{1}{2\mu_{S}}\nabla^{2}\phi+\mu_{S}U\phi\,.

(534)

So, we have showed that, in the Newtonian limit, the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system for
\Phi
and
g_{\mu\nu}
reduces to the Schrödinger-Poisson system for
\phi
and
U
.

Let us now extend our perturbation scheme to first post-Newtonian order. We start by considering the spacetime metric ansatz

\displaystyle g_{00}

\displaystyle=-1-\frac{2}{c^{2}}(U+\delta U)-\frac{2}{c^{4}}\left(\psi+U^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}(c^{-6})\,,

(535)

\displaystyle g_{0j}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{c^{3}}U_{j}+\mathcal{O}(c^{-5})\,,

(536)

\displaystyle g_{jk}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{2}{c^{2}}(U+\delta U)\right)\delta_{jk}+\mathcal{O}(c^{-4})\,,

(537)

with the post-Newtonian terms
U_{j}
,
\psi
and the perturbation
\delta U
. This results in the Ricci tensor components

\displaystyle R_{00}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c^{2}}\nabla^{2}(U+\delta U)+\frac{1}{c^{4}}(3\partial_{t}^{2}U+4U\nabla^{2}U+\nabla^{2}\psi)+\mathcal{O}(c^{-6})\,,

(538)

\displaystyle R_{0j}=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{c^{3}}\nabla^{2}U_{j}+\mathcal{O}(c^{-5})\,,

(539)

\displaystyle R_{jk}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c^{2}}\nabla^{2}(U+\delta U)\delta_{jk}+\mathcal{O}(c^{-4})\,,

(540)

where we imposed the harmonic coordinate condition, which results in

\partial_{t}U+\partial_{j}U^{j}=0\,.

(541)

Now, we introduce a perturbation
\delta\Phi
to the Newtonian scalar field, such that

\delta\Phi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu_{S}}}e^{-i\mu_{S}c^{2}t}\delta\phi\,,

(542)

treated in our perturbation scheme with

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt|\delta\phi|\sim\mathcal{O}(\xi\epsilon^{-2})\,,\quad c^{-1}|\partial_{j}\delta\phi|/|\delta\phi|\sim\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\,,\quad c^{-2}|\partial_{t}\delta\phi|/|\delta\phi|\sim\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\,,

(543)

and
\delta U/c^{2}\sim\mathcal{O}(\xi\epsilon^{-2})
.
Then, the components of the trace-reversed stress-energy tensor of the scalar field are

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt\widetilde{T}_{00}^{S}=\frac{1}{2}\mu_{S}c^{2}|\phi|^{2}+\Im\left(\phi\,\partial_{t}\phi^{*}\right)-\mu_{S}U|\phi|^{2}+\mu_{S}c^{2}\Re\left(\phi^{*}\delta\phi\right)+\mathcal{O}(c^{-2})\,,

(544)

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt\widetilde{T}_{0j}^{S}=c\Im\left(\phi\,\partial_{j}\phi^{*}\right)+\mathcal{O}(c^{-1})\,,

(545)

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt\widetilde{T}_{jk}^{S}=\frac{1}{2}\mu_{S}c^{2}|\phi|^{2}+\mu_{S}c^{2}\Re\left(\phi^{*}\delta\phi\right)+\mathcal{O}(c^{0})\,.

(546)

Thus, it is possible to show that, at first post-Newtonian order, the Einstein equations reduce to

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\psi=8\pi\Big[\Im\left(\phi\,\partial_{t}\phi^{*}\right)-3\mu_{S}U|\phi|^{2}\Big]\,,

(547)

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}U_{j}=4\pi\,\Im\left(\phi\,\partial_{j}\phi^{*}\right)\,,

(548)

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\delta U=8\pi\mu_{S}\,\Re\left(\phi^{*}\delta\phi\right)\,,

(549)

where we used the equations that are satisfied at Newtonian order and we assumed
\partial^{2}_{t}U=0
, since this happens to be always the case in this work.
On the other hand, at next-to-leading order in
\epsilon
(but still in the Newtonian limit), the Klein-Gordon equation reduces to

\displaystyle\hskip-25.6073pti\partial_{t}\delta\phi=-\frac{1}{2\mu_{S}}\nabla^{2}\delta\phi+\mu_{S}U\delta\phi+\mu_{S}\phi\,\delta U+\frac{1}{2\mu_{S}}\partial_{t}^{2}\phi+iU\partial_{t}\phi-\frac{U}{\mu_{S}}\nabla^{2}\phi\,.

(550)

Finally, note that, in the case
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{6})<\mathcal{O}(\xi)<\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{4})
, the last equation becomes simply

i\partial_{t}\delta\phi=-\frac{1}{2\mu_{S}}\nabla^{2}\delta\phi+\mu_{S}U\delta\phi+\mu_{S}\phi\,\delta U\,.

(551)

In the case of a perturbation caused by a point particle, one just needs to include the trace-reversed stress energy tensor of the point particle (
38
) in the Einstein equation (
524
). This is given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}^{p}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\equiv T^{p}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}T^{p}g_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{p}c}{2u^{0}}\left(2u_{\mu}u_{\nu}+g_{\mu\nu}c^{2}\right)\frac{\delta(r-r_{p})}{r^{2}}\frac{\delta(\theta-\theta_{p})}{\sin\theta}\delta(\varphi-\varphi_{p})\,,

(552)

with the particle’s 4-velocity
u^{\mu}\equiv dx_{p}^{\mu}/d\tau
.
We consider that
m_{p}\sim\mathcal{O}(\xi)
and that the particle is non-relativistic, in particular, we consider
u^{i}\sim\sqrt{U}\sim\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
in our perturbation scheme.
Then, the components of the trace-reversed stress-energy tensor of the particle are

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{00}^{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{p}c^{2}}{2}\frac{\delta(r-r_{p})}{r^{2}}\frac{\delta(\theta-\theta_{p})}{\sin\theta}\delta(\varphi-\varphi_{p})+\mathcal{O}(c^{0})\,,

(553)

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{tj}^{p}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(c)\,,\quad\widetilde{T}_{jk}^{p}=\mathcal{O}(c^{2})\,.

(554)

Thus, we conclude that we just need to add an extra term to the last equation in (
549
), which becomes

\nabla^{2}\delta U=4\pi\left[2\mu_{S}\,\Re\left(\phi^{*}\delta\phi\right)+P\right]\,,

(555)

with

P(t,r,\theta,\varphi)\equiv m_{p}\frac{\delta(r-r_{p}(t))}{r^{2}}\frac{\delta(\theta-\theta_{p}(t))}{\sin\theta}\delta(\varphi-\varphi_{p}(t))\,.

(556)

Let us now consider the case of a non-relativistic point particle sourcing ultra-relativistic scalar perturbations to the Newtonian background.
At Newtonian order, the Einstein equations describing the perturbation reduce to the Poisson equation
56
56

56

The assumption of a non-relativistic perturber sourcing an ultra-relativistic scalar perturbation is consistent as long as the scalar is sufficiently light.

\nabla^{2}\delta U=4\pi P\,.

(557)

Finally, at leading order, the Klein-Gordon reduces to

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\delta\Phi-\partial^{2}_{t}\delta\Phi=2\mu_{S}^{2}\Phi\,\delta U\,.

(558)

†
†
margin:

11

Constancy of fundamental matrix determinant

Consider a first-order matrix ordinary differential equation

\frac{d\boldsymbol{X}(r)}{dr}-V(r)\boldsymbol{X}(r)=0\,,

(559)

with
\boldsymbol{X}
a
N
-dimensional column vector and
V
a
N\times N
matrix.
A fundamental matrix of this system is a matrix of the form
F(r)\equiv\big(\boldsymbol{X_{(1)}},...,\boldsymbol{X_{(N)}}\big)
, where
\{\boldsymbol{X_{(1)}},...,\boldsymbol{X_{(N)}}\}
is a set of
N
independent solutions of Eq. (
559
). The determinant of this
N\times N
matrix can be written as

\det F(r)=\epsilon^{i_{1}\,...\,i_{N}}X_{(i_{1})}^{1}\,...\,X_{(i_{N})}^{N}\,,

(560)

where
\epsilon
is the Levi-Civita symbol, and
X_{(k)}^{j}
is the
j
-th component of the vector
\boldsymbol{X_{(k)}}
. Using Eq. (
559
) it is easy to see that

\frac{d}{dr}\det F=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\epsilon^{i_{1}\,...\,i_{N}}V^{k}_{\;\;\;j}\,X_{(i_{1})}^{1}\,...\,X_{(i_{k})}^{j}\,...\,X_{(i_{N})}^{N}\,.

(561)

Using the relation

\epsilon^{i_{1}\,...\,i_{N}}\,X_{(i_{1})}^{1}\,...\,X_{(i_{k})}^{j}\,...\,X_{(i_{N})}^{N}=\delta_{k}^{j}\det F\,,

(562)

one gets

\frac{d}{dr}\det F=\text{Tr}(V)\det F\,.

(563)

If the trace
\text{Tr}(V)\equiv V^{k}_{\;\;\;k}
is identically zero (which is always the case in Part
i
of this thesis), the determinant of the fundamental matrix is constant.

†
†
margin:

12

Scalar field accretion by a static black hole

Appendix 12.A
Incoming flux of energy at the center of a NBS

Here, we compute the incoming flux of energy over a tiny spherical surface at the center of a fundamental
NBS
.
Consider a stationary
NBS
of the form

\Phi=\Psi(r)e^{-i\left(\mu_{S}-\gamma\right)t}\,,

(564)

where
\Psi
is a solution of (
76
) and (
77
).
This stationary field can be written as a sum of incoming and outgoing parts,
\Phi=\Phi_{\text{in}}+\Phi_{\text{out}}
, where

\displaystyle\Phi_{\text{in}}\equiv e^{-i\left(\mu_{S}-\gamma\right)t}\int_{-\infty}^{0}ds\,\overline{\Psi}(s)e^{isr}\,,

\displaystyle\Phi_{\text{out}}\equiv e^{-i\left(\mu_{S}-\gamma\right)t}\int_{0}^{+\infty}ds\,\overline{\Psi}(s)e^{isr}\,,

(565)

with

\overline{\Psi}(s)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}dr\,\Psi(r)e^{-isr}\,,

(566)

and where we are using an even extension of
\Psi
to negative values of
r
. Note that
\overline{\Psi}
is a real-valued function, since
\Psi
is real-valued.
Now, the incoming flux of energy over a tiny spherical surface of radius
r_{+}\ll R
is given by

\dot{E}_{\text{in}}\simeq 4\pi r_{+}^{2}T_{tr}^{\text{in}}(r=0)\,.

(567)

At leading order, one has

\displaystyle T_{tr}^{\text{in}}(r=0)

\displaystyle\simeq\mu_{S}\,\Im\left(\Phi_{\text{in}}\partial_{r}\Phi_{\text{in}}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mu_{S}}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{0}ds^{\prime}\int_{-\infty}^{0}ds\left(s^{\prime}+s\right)\overline{\Psi}(s^{\prime})\overline{\Psi}(s)\,.

(568)

Numerical evaluation of the last expression for a fundamental
NBS
gives

T_{tr}^{\text{in}}(r=0)\sim 2.69\times 10^{-4}\,\mu_{S}^{7}M_{\text{NBS}}^{5}\,.

(569)

Finally, the incoming flux of energy is

\dot{E}_{\text{in}}\sim 3.38\times 10^{-3}\,r_{+}^{2}\mu_{S}^{7}M_{\text{NBS}}^{5}\,.

(570)

Appendix 12.B
Introducing a dissipative boundary

In this section we look at two toy models, aimed at understanding the evolution
of an
NBS
with a small
BH
at its center. The main effect that the
BH
produces is, naturally, dissipation at the horizon. This dissipative boundary condition can also be mimicked with some toy models.

12.b.1
A string absorptive at one end

Here, we wish to study a one-dimensional model of absorption of a scalar structure
when the boundary conditions suddenly change. Consider then a string, initially fixed at
x=0,\,L
, described by the wave equation

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{x}\Phi-\partial^{2}_{t}\Phi=0\,.

(571)

A normal mode satisfying
\Phi(x=0)=\Phi(x=L)=0
is

\displaystyle\Phi=e^{-i\omega_{n}t}\sin\omega_{n}x\,,

(572)

\displaystyle\omega_{n}=\frac{(n+1)\pi}{L}\,,\quad n=0,1,2,...\,.

(573)

We take a configuration with
\omega_{n}=\omega_{0}
and use this as initial data for a problem where the boundary condition at the origin becomes absorptive. In particular, Laplace-transforming the wave equation gives

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\Psi}{dx^{2}}+\omega^{2}\Psi=-\dot{\Phi}(0,x)+i\omega\Phi(0,x)\,,

(574)

\displaystyle\Psi(\omega,x)=\int dte^{i\omega t}\Phi(t,x)\,.

(575)

As boundary conditions, we require that

\displaystyle\Psi(\omega,L)=0\,,\quad\Psi(\omega,x\sim 0)=\sin\omega x-\epsilon e^{-i\omega x}\,.

(576)

These conditions maintain the mirror-like boundary at one extreme
x=L
, while providing an absorption
of energy at
x=0
. The flux of absorbed energy scales like
\epsilon^{2}\ll 1
.
The solution of Eq. (
574
) subjected to the above boundary conditions is

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle=i\frac{\cos^{2}(\omega x)\sin(\pi x/L)+\sin^{2}(\omega x)\sin(\pi x/L)}{\omega-\pi/L}

\displaystyle\qquad+\epsilon\frac{\pi\sin[\omega(L-x)]}{\omega(\pi-L\omega)(i\epsilon\cos(\omega L)+(\epsilon-i)\sin(\omega L))}\,.

(577)

The original time-domain field is given by the inverse

\displaystyle\Phi(t,x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int d\omega e^{-i\omega t}\Psi(\omega,x)\,.

(578)

The integral can be done with the help of the residue theorem. We separate the response
\Phi=\Phi_{1}+\Phi_{2}
. The first term in Eq. (
577
) has a simple,
real
pole
at
\omega=\omega_{0}=\pi/L
, and it evaluates to

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(t,x)=\sin(\pi x/L)e^{-i\pi t/L}\,,

(579)

i. e., it corresponds to the initial data.
The second term has poles at complex values of the frequency, which are also the
QNM
of the dissipative system,

\displaystyle\omega\approx\frac{n\pi+\epsilon-i\epsilon^{2}}{L}\,,

(580)

These poles lie close to the normal modes of the system, including those not present in the initial data.
They dictate an exponential decay
\sim e^{-\epsilon^{2}t}
, and a consequent lifetime
\tau\sim\epsilon^{-2}
.
Note that this simple exercise shows that all modes are excited when new boundary conditions are turned on.
For
NBS
, all the modes cluster around
\omega\sim\mu_{S}
, thus we expect to always be in the low-frequency regime
used to estimate the lifetime.

12.b.2
A black hole in a scalar-filled sphere

Figure 25:

The evolution of a massive scalar field inside a perfectly reflecting spherical surface of radius
R\mu_{S}=20
.
In the center of such a sphere, there sits a
BH
of mass
M_{\text{BH}}\mu_{S}=0.2
(left panels) and
M_{\text{BH}}\mu_{S}=0.1
(right panels).

First row:
Scalar field measured on the horizon.

Second row:
Scalar field measured at
r\mu_{S}=10
.

Third row:
Flux measured at the horizon.

A toy model more similar to the problem we wish to study is that of a
BH
of mass
M_{\text{BH}}
, at the center of a sphere of radius
R
, which was filled with a massive scalar field.
The profile for the scalar is, initially, that of a normal mode (the Klein-Gordon field
\Phi=\Psi/r
),

\displaystyle\Psi=\sin\omega_{0}r\,,

(581)

with
\omega_{0}=\sqrt{\mu_{S}^{2}+\pi^{2}/R^{2}}
. The problem simplifies enormously when the scalar is non self-gravitating and is but a small disturbance in the background of the
BH
spacetime. This is what we assume from now onwards. In such a case all one has to do is evolve the Klein-Gordon equation in a Schwarzschild geometry, subjected to Dirichlet conditions at the surface of the sphere. The results are summarized in Fig.
25
. While they do not mimic entirely the process of accretion of a self-gravitating
NBS
by a central
BH
, these results illustrate some of the possible physics in the more realistic setup.

The figures show the scalar extracted at the horizon (first row), at a midpoint inside the sphere (second raw) and the flux per frequency bin (third raw).
The scalar, measured either at the horizon or somewhere within the sphere, decays exponentially.
The first noteworthy aspect is the sensitive dependence of the decay rate on the size of the
BH
. Our results are consistent with a decay timescale
\tau\sim(M_{\text{BH}}\mu_{S})^{-\beta}
, with
\beta\sim 4-5
, in agreement with our analysis in Section
10
and also with a quasinormal mode ringdown of such fields
[
43
]
.
Note that such suppressed decay for small
M\mu_{S}
couplings happens due to the filtering properties of small
BH
, keeping out most of the low-frequency field.
This also explains why the ratio between the field measured at
r=10
and at the horizon increases when the
BH
size decreases.
Note also that, in accordance with the previous one-dimensional toy model, overtones are also excited. This is clearly seen in the Fourier analysis
(third raw panels in Fig.
25
), showing local peaks at all the subsequent overtones, which were absent in the initial data. These correspond to frequencies
\omega=\sqrt{\mu_{S}^{2}+\pi^{2}n^{2}/R^{2}}\,,n=0,\,1,...
. This is one important difference between this system and
NBS
, for which overtones are all bounded in frequency.

†
†
margin:

13

Scalar Q-balls

Here, we generalize the calculations done in Chapter
4
to Q-balls, where gravity is absent and self-interactions are necessary.

Appendix 13.A
Background configurations

Figure 26:
Three radial profiles
\Psi(r)/\Phi_{c}
obtained through numerical integration of Eq. (
584
) with appropriate boundary conditions (
\Psi(\infty)\rightarrow 0
and
\partial_{r}\Psi(0)=0
). Each curve corresponds to a different Q-ball.

The field equation for
\Phi
is obtained through the variation of action (
2
) with respect to
\Phi^{*}
and reads

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\Phi-2\frac{d\mathcal{U}_{\text{Q}}}{d|\Phi|^{2}}\Phi=0\,,

(582)

where we used
g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}
and the self-interaction potential
\mathcal{U}_{\text{Q}}
defined in Eq. (
36
).
We now look for localized solutions of this model with the form (
35
) – the so-called Q-balls. This ansatz yields the radial equation

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{r}\Psi+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}\Psi+\left[\Omega^{2}-2\frac{d\mathcal{U}_{\text{Q}}}{d|\Phi|^{2}}\right]\Psi=0\,.

(583)

For the class of nonlinear potentials (
36
), the last equation becomes

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{r}\Psi+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}\Psi+\left[\Omega^{2}-\mu^{2}\left(1-\frac{\Psi^{2}}{\Phi_{c}^{2}}\right)\left(1-3\frac{\Psi^{2}}{\Phi_{c}^{2}}\right)\right]\Psi=0\,.

(584)

According to the results of Ref.
[
78
]
, there exist stable Q-ball solutions for any
0<\Omega<\mu_{S}
, independently of the free parameter
\Phi_{c}
.
Additionally, it is known that, in the limit
\Omega/\mu_{S}\ll 1
, the radial function
\Psi
mimics an Heaviside step function (the so-called
thin-wall
Q-balls)
[
78
,
298
,
299
]
. On the other hand, in the regime
\Omega/\mu_{S}\sim 1
, the function
\Psi
starts to fall earlier and drops very slowly (
thick-wall
Q-ball)
[
298
,
299
]
. In particular, using the results of Ref.
[
299
]
one can show that, in the thin-wall limit,

\Psi(r)\simeq\Phi_{c}\left[1+\left(\frac{\Omega}{2\mu_{S}}\right)^{2}\right]\Theta\left(\frac{\mu_{S}}{\Omega^{2}}-r\right)\,.

(585)

Notice that the Q-ball radius is approximately given by
R_{Q}\simeq\mu_{S}/\Omega^{2}
.

A few examples of radial profiles
\Psi(r)
constructed numerically from Eq. (
584
) are shown in Fig.
26
.
From these results it is already evident that, when
\Omega/\mu_{S}\to 0
, the scalar does acquire a Heaviside-type profile. In such a limit the scalar drops to zero on the outside, on a lengthcale

\sim 1/\mu_{S}
. These results also indicate that the radius of the Q-ball grows when
\Omega/\mu_{S}\to 0
. This is made more explicit in
Fig.
27
, showing the numerical results for the dependence of the Q-ball radius
R_{Q}
on the frequency
\Omega
.
57
57

57

We define the Q-ball radius
R_{Q}
to be such that
\dfrac{\Psi(R_{Q})}{\Psi(0)}=1/2
.
The dashed line, corresponding to the thin-wall limit (
585
), agrees remarkably well with the numerics.

Figure 27:
Numerical results for the dependence of the Q-ball radius
R_{Q}\mu_{S}
on the internal frequency
\Omega/\mu_{S}
, obtained through direct integration of Eq. (
584
). The dashed line is the thin-wall limit prediction, Eq. (
585
). A fit on the numerical results gives
R_{Q}\sim 1.08\mu_{S}\Omega^{-2}
, within
2\%
of error, showing a good accordance with the predicted behavior (Eq. (
585
)).

The Q-ball charge
Q
and mass
M_{Q}
are obtained through (
19
) and (
33
), respectively, and read

\displaystyle Q=\frac{4\pi}{\hbar}\Omega\int dr\,r^{2}\Psi^{2}(r)\,,

(586)

\displaystyle M_{Q}=\frac{1}{2}Q\Omega+4\pi\int dr\,r^{2}\left(\frac{(\partial_{r}\Psi)^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{U}(\Psi^{2})\right)\,.

(587)

For thin-wall Q-balls these become

\displaystyle Q=\frac{4\pi}{3}\frac{\Omega^{4}}{\mu_{S}^{6}}\Psi_{c}^{2}\,,

(588)

\displaystyle M_{Q}=\frac{2\pi}{3}\frac{\Omega^{5}}{\mu_{S}^{6}}\Psi_{c}^{2}\,.

(589)

We are using a flat background spacetime, which requires that
M_{Q}/R_{Q}\ll 1
. In the thin-wall limit, this corresponds to

\displaystyle\Omega/\mu_{S}\ll\Psi_{c}^{-2/7}\,.

(590)

Appendix 13.B
Small perturbations

We now wish to understand the effect of a small perturbation on such Q-ball configurations. They can be either (free) sourceless small deformations of the background, or sourced by an external particle. Such perturber could be another (much smaller) Q-ball, or simply some scalar charge piercing the Q-ball or orbiting around it. In the following, the external probe is modelled as point-like, which means that our results are valid only for objects whose spatial extent are
\ll R_{\text{Q}}
.
We consider an interaction between the perturber and the Q-ball described by the action (
2
) with
J_{S}=T_{p}
, where
T_{p}\equiv\eta_{\mu\nu}T_{p}^{\mu\nu}
is the trace of the particle’s energy-momentum tensor defined in (
38
). This coupling allows for
EOM
that are both simple enough to be handled via our perturbation scheme, described in Chapter
3
, and that show interesting dynamical features, as we shall see later. In the present analysis, we neglect the backreaction on the particle motion, therefore, the particle’s world-line
x_{p}^{\mu}(\tau)
is considered to be known.

An external particle sources a scalar field fluctuation of the form (
37
) in the Q-ball background, which satisfies the linearized equation

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\delta\Psi-\partial_{t}^{2}\delta\Psi+\left[\Omega^{2}-\mu_{S}^{2}\left(1-8\frac{\Psi^{2}}{\Phi_{c}^{2}}+9\frac{\Psi^{4}}{\Phi_{c}^{4}}\right)\right]\delta\Psi

\displaystyle\qquad+2i\Omega\partial_{t}\delta\Psi+2\mu_{S}^{2}\frac{\Psi^{2}}{\Phi_{c}^{2}}\left(2-3\frac{\Psi^{2}}{\Phi_{c}^{2}}\right)\delta\Psi^{*}=T_{p}e^{i\Omega t}\,.

(591)

The sourceless case is recovered simply by setting
T_{p}=0
.
Decomposing the particle stress-energy trace as

\displaystyle T_{p}e^{i\Omega t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l,m}\int\frac{d\omega}{\sqrt{2\pi}r}\Big[T_{1}^{\omega lm}Y_{l}^{m}e^{-i\omega t}+\left(T_{2}^{\omega lm}\right)^{*}\left(Y_{l}^{m}\right)^{*}e^{i\omega t}\Big]\,,

(592)

where
T_{1}^{\omega lm}
and
T_{2}^{\omega lm}
are radial complex-functions defined by

\displaystyle T_{1}^{\omega lm}\equiv\frac{r}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}\int dtd\theta d\varphi\sin\theta\,T_{p}e^{i(\omega+\Omega)t}\left(Y_{l}^{m}\right)^{*}\,,\hskip 14.22636pt

(593)

\displaystyle T_{2}^{\omega lm}\equiv\frac{r}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}\int dtd\theta d\varphi\sin\theta\,T_{p}e^{i(\omega-\Omega)t}\left(Y_{l}^{m}\right)^{*}\,.\hskip 14.22636pt

(594)

Plugging the decompositions (
40
) and (
592
) in Eq. (
591
), one obtains the matrix equation
58
58

58

The symmetry of this system implies that the radial functions satisfy
Z_{2}(\omega,l;r)=Z_{1}(-\omega,l;r)^{*}
. The functions
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
are clearly independent of the azimuthal number
m
.

\partial_{r}\boldsymbol{Z}-V_{Q}(r)\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{T}\,,

(595)

with the vector
\boldsymbol{Z}\equiv(Z_{1},Z_{2},\partial_{r}Z_{1},\partial_{r}Z_{2})^{T}
, the matrix
V_{Q}
given by

V_{Q}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1&0\\
0&0&0&1\\
V_{s}-(\omega+\Omega)^{2}&V_{c}&0&0\\
V_{c}&V_{s}-(\omega-\Omega)^{2}&0&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

where we defined the radial potentials

\displaystyle V_{s}(r)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{l(l+1)}{r^{2}}+\mu_{S}^{2}\left(1-8\frac{\Psi_{0}^{2}}{\Phi_{c}^{2}}+9\frac{\Psi_{0}^{4}}{\Phi_{c}^{4}}\right)\,,

(596)

\displaystyle V_{c}(r)

\displaystyle\equiv-2\mu_{S}^{2}\frac{\Psi_{0}^{2}}{\Phi_{c}^{2}}\left(2-3\frac{\Psi_{0}^{2}}{\Phi_{c}^{2}}\right)\,.

(597)

and the source term
59
59

59

To simplify the notation, we omit the labels
\omega
,
l
and
m
in the functions
T_{1}^{\omega lm}
and
T_{2}^{\omega lm}
.

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{T}(r)\equiv\big(0,0,T_{1},T_{2}\big)^{T}\,.

(598)

To solve for the small perturbations, either in the sourced or sourceless case, we need to establish suitable boundary conditions. We require regular solutions at the origin,

\boldsymbol{Z}(r\to 0)\sim\left(ar^{l+1},br^{l+1},a(l+1)r^{l},b(l+1)r^{l}\right)^{T}\,,

with (complex) constants
a
and
b
, and the Sommerfeld radiation condition at infinity

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z}(r\to\infty)\sim\left(Z_{1}^{\infty}e^{ik_{1}r},Z_{2}^{\infty}e^{ik_{2}r},ik_{1}Z_{1}^{\infty}e^{ik_{1}r},ik_{2}Z_{2}^{\infty}e^{ik_{2}r}\right)\,,

(599)

with

\displaystyle k_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv\epsilon_{1}\sqrt{\left(\omega+\Omega\right)^{2}-\mu_{S}^{2}}\,,

(600)

\displaystyle k_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\epsilon_{2}\left(\sqrt{\left(\omega-\Omega\right)^{2}-\mu_{S}^{2}}\right)^{*}\,,

(601)

where we are using the principal complex square root.

Consider then the set of independent solutions
\{\boldsymbol{Z_{(1)}},\boldsymbol{Z_{(2)}},\boldsymbol{Z_{(3)}},\boldsymbol{Z_{(4)}}\}
uniquely determined by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z_{(1)}}(r\to 0)

\displaystyle\sim\Big(r^{l+1},0,(l+1)r^{l},0\Big)^{T}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z_{(2)}}(r\to 0)

\displaystyle\sim\Big(0,r^{l+1},0,(l+1)r^{l}\Big)^{T}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z_{(3)}}(r\to\infty)

\displaystyle\sim\Big(e^{ik_{1}r},0,ik_{1}e^{ik_{1}r},0\Big)^{T}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z_{(4)}}(r\to\infty)

\displaystyle\sim\Big(0,e^{ik_{2}r},0,ik_{2}e^{ik_{2}r}\Big)^{T}\,.

(602)

The
4\times 4
matrix
F(r)\equiv\big(\boldsymbol{Z_{(1)}},\boldsymbol{Z_{(2)}},\boldsymbol{Z_{(3)}},\boldsymbol{Z_{(4)}}\big)
is the fundamental matrix of the system (
595
). As shown in Appendix
11
, for a system of the form (
595
), the determinant
\text{det}(F)
is independent of
r
.

13.b.1
Sourceless perturbations

Free oscillations of Q-ball configurations are regular scalar fluctuations satisfying the Sommerfeld radiation condition at infinity. They correspond to scalar perturbations of the form

\delta\Psi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}r}\left[Z_{1}Y_{l}^{m}e^{-i\omega t}+Z_{2}^{*}\left(Y_{l}^{m}\right)^{*}e^{i\omega^{*}t}\right]\,,

(603)

where
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
are solutions of system (
595
) with
\boldsymbol{T}=0
. For complex-valued
\omega
, the free oscillations are
QNM
. For a real
\omega
, these are termed normal modes. Notice that for the discrete set
\{\omega_{\text{QNM}}\}
of
QNM
frequencies, the solutions
\{\boldsymbol{Z_{(1)}},\boldsymbol{Z_{(2)}},\boldsymbol{Z_{(3)}},\boldsymbol{Z_{(4)}}\}
are not linearly independent. In fact, it is easy to see that the condition
\text{det}(F)=0
holds if and only if
\omega
is a
QNM
frequency (i. e.,
\omega\in\{\omega_{\text{QNM}}\}
).

13.b.2
External perturbers

Let us now turn to the perturbations induced by an external particle, which interacts with the background scalar field. How is such a body exciting the Q-ball, how much radiation does the interaction give rise to, what backreaction does the Q-ball
exert on the perturber? These are all questions that can be raised in this context, and that we wish to answer here.
In order to do that, one needs to find the solutions of system (
595
) that are regular at the origin and satisfy the Sommerfeld condition at infinity. These can be obtained through the method of variation of parameters,

\displaystyle Z_{1}(r)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=3}^{4}\Bigg[\sum_{n=1}^{2}F_{1,n}(r)\int_{\infty}^{r}dr^{\prime}F^{-1}_{n,k}\boldsymbol{T}_{k}+\sum_{n=3}^{4}F_{1,n}(r)\int_{0}^{r}dr^{\prime}F^{-1}_{n,k}\boldsymbol{T}_{k}\Bigg]\,,

(604)

\displaystyle Z_{2}(r)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=3}^{4}\Bigg[\sum_{n=1}^{2}F_{2,n}(r)\int_{\infty}^{r}dr^{\prime}F^{-1}_{n,k}\boldsymbol{T}_{k}+\sum_{n=3}^{4}F_{2,n}(r)\int_{0}^{r}dr^{\prime}F^{-1}_{n,k}\boldsymbol{T}_{k}\Bigg]\,.

(605)

The total scalar field energy, linear and angular momenta radiated during a given process can be found using solely the amplitudes
Z_{1}^{\infty}
and
Z_{2}^{\infty}
. These are given by

\displaystyle Z_{1}^{\infty}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=3}^{4}\int_{0}^{\infty}dr^{\prime}F^{-1}_{3,k}(r^{\prime})\boldsymbol{T}_{k}(r^{\prime})\,,

(606)

\displaystyle Z_{2}^{\infty}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=3}^{4}\int_{0}^{\infty}dr^{\prime}F^{-1}_{4,k}(r^{\prime})\boldsymbol{T}_{k}(r^{\prime})\,.

(607)

Let us now apply our framework to two physically interesting setups: a particle plunging into a Q-ball configuration, and a particle in a circular orbit within the Q-ball.

Plunging particle.

Consider a particle moving at a constant velocity
\boldsymbol{v}=-v\boldsymbol{e}_{z}
(with
v>0
), plunging into a Q-ball, and crossing its center at
t=0
.
In this case, the trace of the particle’s energy-momentum tensor reads

\displaystyle T_{p}=

\displaystyle-\left[\delta\left(r+vt\right)\delta\left(\theta\right)\Theta(-t)+\delta\left(r-vt\right)\delta\left(\theta-\pi\right)\Theta(t)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\times m_{p}\,\delta(\varphi)\frac{\sqrt{1-v^{2}}}{r^{2}\sin\theta}\,.

(608)

Therefore, the source decompositions (
593
) and (
594
) read

\displaystyle T_{1}=

\displaystyle-\left[\cos\left(\frac{(\omega+\Omega)r}{v}\right)\delta_{l}^{\text{even}}-i\sin\left(\frac{(\omega+\Omega)r}{v}\right)\delta_{l}^{\text{odd}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\times m_{p}\,Y_{l}^{0}(0,0)\delta_{m}^{0}\frac{\sqrt{1-v^{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}rv}\,,

(609)

\displaystyle T_{2}=

\displaystyle-\left[\cos\left(\frac{(\omega-\Omega)r}{v}\right)\delta_{l}^{\text{even}}-i\sin\left(\frac{(\omega-\Omega)r}{v}\right)\delta_{l}^{\text{odd}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\times m_{p}\,Y_{l}^{0}(0,0)\delta_{m}^{0}\frac{\sqrt{1-v^{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}rv}\,.

(610)

These satisfy the property

\displaystyle T_{2}(\omega,l,0;r)=T_{1}(-\omega,l,0;r)^{*}\,.

(611)

Thus, due to the form of the system (
595
), one has

\displaystyle Z_{2}(\omega,l,0;r)=Z_{1}(-\omega,l,0;r)^{*}\,,

(612)

\displaystyle Z_{2}^{\infty}(\omega,l,0)=Z_{1}^{\infty}(-\omega,l,0)^{*}\,.

(613)

Finally, the spectral fluxes (
46
), (
52
) and (
57
) become, respectively,

\displaystyle\frac{dE^{\text{rad}}}{d\omega}

\displaystyle=4\left|\omega+\Omega\right|\Re\left[\sqrt{(\omega+\Omega)^{2}-\mu_{S}^{2}}\right]\sum_{l}\left|Z_{1}^{\infty}(\omega,l,0)\right|^{2}\,,

(614)

\displaystyle\frac{dP_{z}^{\text{rad}}}{d\omega}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\frac{8(l+1)\Theta\left[\left(\omega+\Omega\right)^{2}-\mu_{S}^{2}\right]\left|(\omega+\Omega)^{2}-\mu_{S}^{2}\right|}{\sqrt{(2l+1)(2l+3)}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Re\left[Z_{1}^{\infty}(\omega,l,0)Z_{1}^{\infty}(\omega,l+1,0)^{*}\right]\,,

(615)

\displaystyle\frac{dL_{z}^{\text{rad}}}{d\omega}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(616)

Orbiting particle

Now we consider a system composed by a particle describing a circular orbit of radius
r_{\text{orb}}
and angular frequency
\omega_{\text{orb}}
inside a Q-ball and in its equatorial plane. The trace of the particle’s stress-energy tensor is

\displaystyle T_{p}

\displaystyle=-\frac{m_{p}}{r_{\text{orb}}^{2}}\sqrt{1-\left(\omega_{\text{orb}}r_{\text{orb}}\right)^{2}}\,\delta(r-r_{\text{orb}})\delta\left(\theta-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\delta(\varphi-\omega_{\text{orb}}t)\,,

(617)

which implies

\displaystyle T_{1,2}

\displaystyle=-m_{p}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\,Y_{l}^{m}\left(\frac{\pi}{2},0\right)\frac{\sqrt{1-\left(\omega_{\text{orb}}r_{\text{orb}}\right)^{2}}}{r_{\text{orb}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\delta\left(r-r_{\text{orb}}\right)\delta\left(\omega\pm\Omega-m\omega_{\text{orb}}\right)\,.

(618)

Notice that
T_{2}(\omega,l,m)=(-1)^{m}T_{1}(-\omega,l,-m)
, hence due to the form of system (
595
), we have

\displaystyle Z_{2}(\omega,l,m;r)=(-1)^{m}Z_{1}(-\omega,l,-m;r)^{*}\,,

(619)

\displaystyle Z_{2}^{\infty}(\omega,l,m)=(-1)^{m}Z_{1}^{\infty}(-\omega,l,-m)^{*}\,.

(620)

Then, the emission rate expressions (
47
) and (
58
) imply, omitting the arguments
(\omega,l,m)
,

\displaystyle\dot{E}^{\text{rad}}=\frac{2}{\pi}\int d\omega\left|\omega+\Omega\right|\Re\left[\sqrt{(\omega+\Omega)^{2}-\mu_{S}^{2}}\right]\sum_{l,m}\left|Z_{1}^{\infty}\right|^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\dot{L}_{z}^{\text{rad}}=\frac{2}{\pi}\int d\omega\,\epsilon_{1}(\omega)\Re\left[\sqrt{(\omega+\Omega)^{2}-\mu_{S}^{2}}\right]\sum_{l,m}m\left|Z_{1}^{\infty}\right|^{2}.

where we remind that
\epsilon_{1}\equiv\text{sign}(\omega+\Omega+\mu_{S})
. Re-writing expression (
618
) in the form

\displaystyle T_{1,2}=\widetilde{T}(\omega_{\text{orb}},r_{\text{orb}})\,\delta\left(r-r_{\text{orb}}\right)\delta\left(\omega\pm\Omega-m\omega_{\text{orb}}\right)\,,

(621)

the previous expressions for the rate of emission read

\displaystyle\dot{E}^{\text{rad}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{l,m}\widetilde{T}^{2}\Big[a_{1}\left|F_{3,3}^{-1}\left(m\omega_{\text{orb}}-\Omega;\,r_{\text{orb}}\right)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+a_{2}\left|F_{3,4}^{-1}\left(m\omega_{\text{orb}}+\Omega;\,r_{\text{orb}}\right)\right|^{2}\Big]\,,

(622)

\displaystyle\dot{L}_{z}^{\text{rad}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{l,m}m\widetilde{T}^{2}\Big[\epsilon_{1}a_{1}\left|F_{3,3}^{-1}\left(m\omega_{\text{orb}}-\Omega;\,r_{\text{orb}}\right)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\epsilon_{1}a_{2}\left|F_{3,4}^{-1}\left(m\omega_{\text{orb}}+\Omega;\,r_{\text{orb}}\right)\right|^{2}\Big]\,.

(623)

where

\displaystyle a_{1}=|m\omega_{\text{orb}}|\Re\left[\sqrt{\left(m\omega_{\text{orb}}\right)^{2}-\mu_{S}^{2}}\right]\,,

\displaystyle a_{2}=\left|m\omega_{\text{orb}}+2\Omega\right|\Re\left[\sqrt{\left(m\omega_{\text{orb}}+2\Omega\right)^{2}-\mu_{S}^{2}}\right].

(624)

Appendix 13.C
Free oscillations

The numerical search for
QNM
frequencies for Q-balls is summarized in Table
3
, for the particular configuration with
\Omega=0.3\mu_{S}
. When
\omega_{\text{QNM}}
are pure real numbers, they are normal modes of the object. For a mode to be normal, it must not be dispersed to infinity, hence the condition
\omega<\mu_{S}-\Omega
is necessary, which also implies that such modes are screened from far-away observers by the Q-ball background itself. This means that perturbations associated with the real-valued frequencies in Table
3
do not reach spatial infinity. Such modes are the analogs of the
NBS
modes found in Chapter
4
, which were
all
normal (Table
1
). Q-balls have, in addition to such modes, also quasi-normal modes, which decay in time since they have a sufficiently large energy to disperse to infinity.

l

\omega_{\text{QNM}}/\mu_{S}

0

0.439

0.689

0.931-1.2\times 10^{-4}i

1.153-1.6\times 10^{-2}i

1

0.300

0.555

0.806-9.8\times 10^{-4}i

1.04-3.3\times 10^{-3}i

Table 3:
Some
QNM
frequencies of a Q-ball configuration with
\Omega/\mu_{S}=0.3
, for
l=\{0,1,2\}
. Note that the first column corresponds to normal modes, with
\omega<\mu_{S}
, hence screened from distant observers; they are confined to a spatial extent
\sim R_{\text{Q}}
, the radius of the Q-ball (these modes are the analogs of the
NBS
modes in Table
1
). There is an infinity of
QNM
frequencies, parametrized by an integer overtone index
n
. At large
n
,
\Re\left(\omega_{\text{QNM}}\right)\sim 0.22n\sim\pi n/R_{\text{Q}}
, as might be anticipated by a WKB analysis. Our results for the imaginary part of
\omega_{\text{QNM}}
carry a large uncertainty, and should be taken only as an order of magnitude estimate.

13.c.1
Particles plunging into Q-balls

Figure 28:
Energy spectra of scalar radiation emitted when a particle of rest-mass
m_{p}
plunges through a Q-ball with
\Omega=0.3\mu_{S}
, with a large velocity
v=0.8c
.
The spectrum was decomposed into multipoles (Eq. (
614
)).
The sharp peaks correspond to the excitation of
QNM
frequencies
\omega_{\text{QNM}}
(Table
3
).

Figure 29:
Linear momentum spectra of scalar radiation emitted when a particle plunges through a Q-ball with
\Omega=0.3\mu_{S}
, with a velocity
v=0.8
.
Different lines correspond to the different multipolar cross-terms in Eq. (
615
).

For concreteness, here we restrict the discussion to a large-velocity plunge
v=0.8c
.
The multipolar energy spectrum
dE^{\text{rad}}_{l}/d\omega
of radiation released during such process is shown in Fig.
28
for the first lowest multipoles, obtained through numerical evaluation of Eq. (
614
). Just like a hammer hitting a bell excites its characteristic vibration modes, the effect of a plunging particle is to excite the
QNM
of a Q-ball.
Figure
28
illustrates this feature very clearly, the peaks in the energy spectrum are all coincident with the
QNM
, some of them identified in Table
3
. This feature was absent in the dynamics of
NBS
simply because the modes of
NBS
(Table
1
) are all normal and confined within the
NBS
; they do not arrive at spatial infinity. We see that most of the radiation is dipolar, looking at Fig.
28
, but a substantial amount is also emitted in higher multipoles.
For example, the
l=4
mode still carries roughly
10\%
of the total radiated energy. Our results are compatible with an exponential suppression at large
l
, of the form

E^{\text{rad}}_{l}\sim 0.085e^{-0.39l}
. We can use this to sum over all multipoles and find the total energy radiated,

\displaystyle E^{\text{rad}}\sim 0.188\,m_{p}^{2}\,\mu_{S}\,.

(625)

The emitted radiation carries linear momentum, which is due to an interference term between multipoles (Eq. (
615
)).
Figure
29
shows the contribution of the multipoles
l\leq 4
to the spectral flux of linear momentum
dP_{z}^{\text{rad}}/d\omega
, obtained through numerical evaluation of (
615
). Again, most of the contribution comes from the excitation of the Q-ball’s
QNM
. Note the interesting feature that, although not shown in the figures, in some frequency ranges and for some interference terms, the radiated momentum is actually positive along
\boldsymbol{e}_{z}
, i. e., opposite to the direction of the motion.
We observed numerically that the total flux of linear momentum
P_{z}^{\text{rad}}
converge exponentially in
l
, for sufficiently large
l
. The total radiated momentum is always negative, and, thus, represents a slowing-down of the moving point particle. Using a similar fitting procedure to sum over all multipoles, we find for this particular configuration,

\displaystyle P^{\text{rad}}\sim-0.088\,m_{p}^{2}\,\mu_{S}\,.

(626)

13.c.2
Orbiting particles

Figure 30:
Average dipolar (
|m|=l=1
) rate of energy (up), and angular momentum (down) radiated by a particle describing a circular orbit around a Q-ball with
\Omega=0.3\mu_{S}
, at radius
r_{\text{orb}}\mu_{S}=1/3
and with orbital frequency
\omega_{\text{orb}}
. The peaks are associated with the excitation of
QNM
frequencies
\omega_{\text{QNM}}
for
\omega_{\text{orb}}=\Re\left(\omega_{\text{QNM}}\right)\pm\Omega
– each
QNM
frequency is excited by two different
\omega_{\text{orb}}
spaced by
2\Omega
. The excitation of the
QNM
frequencies with
\Re(\omega_{\text{QNM}})=\{0.806,\,1.04
(in Table
3
)
,\,1.298\}\mu_{S}
is clearly seen from these plots. However, it seems that not all the
QNM
frequencies can be efficiently excited; e. g.,
\Re(\omega_{\text{QNM}})/\mu_{S}=2.30
(in Tab.
3
).

Figure 31:
Average rate of energy radiated by a particle describing a circular orbit around a Q-ball with
\Omega=0.3\mu_{S}
, at a radius
r_{\text{orb}}\mu_{S}=1/3
and with orbital frequency
\omega_{\text{orb}}
for different values of
l=m
. At low frequencies the radiation is mostly dipolar. At large orbital frequencies the radiation is synchrotron-like and peaked at large
l=m
. In the high-frequency regime, there is a critical multipole
m
beyond which the energy radiated decreases exponentially
(see main text for further details).
There are QNM peaks for all multipoles, but they are visible only for the dipolar and quadrupolar.

The average dipolar flux of energy and angular momentum emitted by a particle in circular orbit inside a Q-ball (
\Omega=0.3\mu_{S}
), at an orbital distance
r_{\text{orb}}\mu_{S}=1/3
,
are shown in Fig.
30
. The point-like source is assumed to be orbiting due to some external force, and its orbital frequency is varied, scanning possible resonant behavior with the Q-ball. As expected, and verified numerically, the quantity
\dot{E}^{\text{rad}}
is an even function of
\omega_{\text{orb}}
, whereas
\dot{L}_{z}^{\text{rad}}
is an odd one. A few features are apparent in the results above (obtained evaluating Eqs. (
622
)-(
623
)).
The fluxes have clear peaks, which correspond to the resonant excitation of the
QNM
of the Q-ball. It is worth to note that for each
QNM
frequency listed in Table
3
there are two peaks associated with different orbital frequencies separated by a distance
2\Omega
; the resonances now occur at
\omega_{\text{orb}}=\Omega\pm\omega_{\text{QNM}}
. This comes directly from the decomposition (
592
).

In flat space, a scalar charge on a circular orbit also emits radiation
[
300
,
222
]
. For small orbital frequencies and massless fields, the flux is dipolar and of order
\dot{E}\sim q^{2}r_{\text{orb}}^{2}\omega_{\text{orb}}^{4}/(12\pi)

[
300
,
222
]
(with a scalar charge
q=m_{p}
, in our coupling).
This explains the rise of the dipolar flux when the orbital frequency increases. However, at large frequencies, the radiation becomes of synchrotron type, and it is emitted preferentially in higher multipoles
[
301
,
302
]
. This is apparent in Fig.
31
where we show the contribution of higher multipoles to the flux.
Note that all other multipoles also have resonant peaks, but these are less pronounced than the dipolar. At large Lorentz factors
\gamma
, there is a critical
m
mode after which the fluxes becomes exponentially suppressed. The critical multipole is of order
m_{\text{crit}}\propto\gamma^{2}

[
301
,
302
]
. Thus an evaluation of a large number of multipoles is necessary to have an accurate estimate of fluxes at large velocities. Our results are consistent with such a prediction. We find that as
\omega_{\text{orb}}
increases, the flux peaks at higher and higher
m
, but there is always a threshold
m
beyond which the radiation output is exponentially suppressed. Finally, since this process is not axially symmetric, one cannot use expression (
52
) to compute the flux of linear momentum along
z
. Nevertheless, it is straightforward to show that the average rate of linear momentum radiated
\dot{P}_{z}^{\text{rad}}
vanishes.

Figure 32:
Average rate of energy radiated by a particle describing a circular orbit around a Q-ball with
\Omega=0.7\mu_{S}
, at radius
r_{\text{orb}}\mu_{S}=1/3
and with orbital frequency
\omega_{\text{orb}}
. For such a scalar configuration there is radiation emitted also in the monopole mode, and it dominates the emission, as seen in the inset.

Figure 33:
Average rate of energy radiated in the case of a particle standing at a fixed radius
r_{\text{orb}}\mu_{S}=1/3
as function of
\Omega/\mu_{S}
. It is shown the dominant contributions from the modes
l=0
and
l=1
. The average rate of angular momentum radiated in this case vanishes.

One interesting aspect, not seen in the study of
NBS
, concerns
monopolar
emission and emission from particles
at rest
. Both features are usually absent.
It follows from Eq. (
622
), that for Q-ball configurations with
\Omega\leq\mu_{S}/2
there is no emission of
l=0
, and the first mode contributing to the radiation is
l=1
. For these objects there is no radiation emitted if the particle is at rest, with
\omega_{\text{orb}}=0
. However, for Q-balls
with
\Omega/\mu_{S}>1/2
there is indeed emission from
l=0
modes, contributing more than (or, at least as much as) the
l=1
modes to the radiation (Fig.
32
). Interestingly, for these Q-balls there is also radiation emitted even when the particle is at rest (Fig.
33
). This type of behavior is due to the
scalar
coupling between two dynamical entities: the external perturber (through
T_{p}
) and the Q-ball configuration (through
\Phi
). The different coupling (purely gravitational, with no scalar charge) considered in the treatment of
NBS
led to the absence of these features.

†
†
margin:

14

Green’s Function of Gaseous Slabs

Here we derive expression (
337
). The Green’s function
G(t,\boldsymbol{r};t^{\prime},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})
of a three-dimensional gaseous slab is a solution of

\nabla_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{2}G-\frac{1}{c^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial t^{2}}=-\delta(t-t^{\prime})\delta^{(3)}(\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})\,,

(627)

with Dirichlet boundary conditions

G(t,x,y,z=\pm L;\,t^{\prime},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})=G(t,\boldsymbol{r};\,t^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime}=\pm L)=0\,.

(628)

Since the slab is homogeneous in the
x
and
y
directions, the Green’s function is translation-invariant in these directions and can only depend on
\boldsymbol{R}=(x-x^{\prime},y-y^{\prime})
. In the same way, since the medium was assumed to be static, the Green’s function is time translation-invariant and only depends on
T=t-t^{\prime}
.
Now, note that the most general function of
T
,
\boldsymbol{R}
,
z
and
z^{\prime}
satisfying (
628
) can be expanded as

\displaystyle G=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}L}\sum_{n,n^{\prime}>0}\int d\omega\,

\displaystyle d^{2}\boldsymbol{K}\,\widetilde{G}_{nn^{\prime}}(\omega,\boldsymbol{K})e^{-i(\omega T-\boldsymbol{K}\cdot\boldsymbol{R})}

\displaystyle\times\sin[m_{n^{\prime}}(z^{\prime}+L)]\sin[m_{n}(z+L)]\,,

(629)

with
m_{n}=n\pi/(2L)
. Using the relations

\displaystyle\delta(T)\delta^{2}(\boldsymbol{R})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int d\omega\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{K}\,e^{-i(\omega T-\boldsymbol{K}\cdot\boldsymbol{R})}\,,

(630)

\displaystyle\delta(z-z^{\prime})=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{n>0}\sin[m_{n}(z^{\prime}+L)]\sin[m_{n}(z+L)]\,,

(631)

where the second expression is the
completeness relation
of the orthonormal basis
\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\sin[m_{n}(z+L)]\}_{n>0}
of the function space
\mathbb{L}^{2}(-L,L)
with Dirichlet conditions at the boundaries.
60
60

60

The completeness relation comes directly from the fact that any function
f(z)
in
\mathbb{L}^{2}(-L,L)
with Dirichlet conditions at the boundaries can be expanded as

\displaystyle f(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\sum_{n>0}\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\int_{-L}^{L}dz^{\prime}f(z^{\prime})\sin[m_{n}(z^{\prime}+L)]\right\}\sin[m_{n}(z+L)]\,.

Interchanging the order of the sum and the integration one arrives at the relation.

Then, Eq. (
337
) implies

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}=\frac{\delta_{nn^{\prime}}}{k^{2}+m_{n}^{2}-\left(\frac{\omega}{c}\right)^{2}}\,.

(632)

Now, note that

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76228pt\int d\omega d^{2}\boldsymbol{K}\frac{e^{-i(\omega T-\boldsymbol{K}\cdot\boldsymbol{R})}}{K^{2}+m_{n}^{2}-\left(\frac{\omega}{c}\right)^{2}}=-2\pi c^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}dK\,KJ_{0}(KR)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega\frac{e^{-i\omega T}}{\omega^{2}-\omega_{K}^{2}}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{2}c^{2}\,\Theta(T)\int_{0}^{\infty}dK\,KJ_{0}(KR)\frac{\sin(\omega_{K}T)}{\omega_{K}}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{2}c\,\Theta(cT-R)\frac{\cos\Big(m_{n}\sqrt{(cT)^{2}-R^{2}}\Big)}{\sqrt{(cT)^{2}-R^{2}}}\,,

(633)

where
\omega_{K}=c\sqrt{K^{2}+m_{n}^{2}}
and
J_{0}
is a Bessel function of the first kind. The first equality results from the integration in the angle between
\boldsymbol{K}
and
\boldsymbol{R}
(note that both vectors are two-dimensional), the second equality results from an integration in
\omega
over the complex-plane using Cauchy’s integral formula, and for the third equality we used formula
6.737.5.
of Ref.
[
303
]
.

Finally, plugging (
632
) back in the Fourier expansion (
14
) and using the result of the last integration one finds

\displaystyle\hskip-17.07182ptG=\frac{c}{2\pi L}\Theta(cT-R)\sum_{n>0}\frac{\cos(m_{n}D)}{D}\sin[m_{n}(z^{\prime}+L)]\sin[m_{n}(z+L)]\,,

(634)

where
D=\sqrt{(cT)^{2}-R^{2}}
. That is exactly expression (
337
). All the steps could be repeated in a similar fashion to derive

\displaystyle\hskip-17.07182ptG=\frac{c}{2\pi L}\Theta(cT-R)\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{\cos(m_{n}D)}{D}\cos[m_{n}(z^{\prime}+L)]\cos[m_{n}(z+L)]\,

(635)

for Neumann boundary conditions.

†
†
margin:

15

Scalar and vector charged binaries

In addition to
GW
emission, many theories predict that binaries could also emit through other channels, such as scalar and vector radiation. These additional emission can take place, for instance, if the
BH
composing the binaries have scalar charges, as it is the case for self-interacting scalar fields, or even electromagnetic charges, as predicted by the Kerr-Newman class of
BH
. In what follows, we explore the consequences of additional radiative sectors for the evolution of binaries.

15..1
Scalar charged

15..1.1
The theory

Consider the theory (
2
) with currents

\displaystyle J_{S}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{2}q_{n}^{0}\int d\tau_{n}\frac{\delta^{(4)}\left(x^{\alpha}-x_{n}^{\;\;\alpha}(\tau_{n})\right)}{\sqrt{-g}}\,,

(636)

\displaystyle J_{V}^{\;\;\alpha}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(637)

describing a massless real scalar field sourced by two particles moving on a curved spacetime with metric
g_{\alpha\beta}
.
Here
x_{n}^{\;\;\alpha}(\tau_{n})
is the world-line of the particle
n=\{1,2\}
parametrized by its proper time
\tau_{n}
. Particle
n
has mass and scalar charge, respectively,
m_{n}
and
q^{0}_{n}
.
This theory has been extensively studied in the past (see, e.g., Refs.
[
304
,
305
]
).

The Einstein
EOM
(
7
) is

\displaystyle G^{\alpha\beta}=8\pi G

\displaystyle\Bigg[T_{S}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}+\sum_{n=1}^{2}(m_{n}+q^{0}_{n}\Phi)

\displaystyle\times\int d\tau_{n}\,\frac{dx_{n}^{\;\;\alpha}}{d\tau_{n}}\frac{dx_{n}^{\;\;\beta}}{d\tau_{n}}\frac{\delta^{(4)}\left(x^{\delta}-x_{n}^{\;\;\delta}(\tau_{n})\right)}{{\sqrt{-g}}}\Bigg]\,,

(638)

with the scalar energy-momentum tensor given in (
10
), the scalar
EOM
(
5
) is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\alpha}\left(\sqrt{-g}\,g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\Phi\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{2}q_{n}^{0}\int d\tau_{n}\frac{\delta^{(4)}\left(x^{\alpha}-x_{n}^{\;\;\alpha}(\tau_{n})\right)}{\sqrt{-g}}\,,

(639)

and
EOM
of the particles is

\displaystyle\left(m_{n}+q^{0}_{n}\Phi\right)u_{n}^{\;\;\alpha}\nabla_{\alpha}u_{n}^{\;\;\beta}=-q^{0}_{n}\left(g^{\alpha\beta}+u_{n}^{\;\;\alpha}u_{n}^{\;\;\beta}\right)\Phi_{,\alpha}\,,

(640)

where
u_{n}^{\alpha}\equiv dx_{n}^{\;\;\alpha}/d\tau_{n}
is the 4-velocity of particle
n
.

15..1.2
Newtonian binary with no radiation

Consider a slowly-moving, Newtonian binary, such that energy and angular momentum fluxes can be neglected at leading order. In this limit Eq. (
15..1.1
) becomes a simple Poisson equation
[
72
]

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}U=4\pi G\sum_{n=1}^{2}m_{j}\delta^{(3)}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{r}_{n}(t))\,,

(641)

where
x_{n}^{\;\;\alpha}\equiv(t,\boldsymbol{r}_{n}(t))
. The gravitational potential
U(t,\boldsymbol{x})
is weak,
i.e.

|U|\ll 1
, and enters in the Newtonian metric

\displaystyle ds^{2}=-(1+2U)dt^{2}+dr^{2}+r^{2}\left(d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi^{2}\right)\,.

(642)

There is a (slowly time-varying) scalar field sourced by the point charges described by Eq. (
639
), which in this limit becomes also a Poisson equation

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\Phi_{0}=\sum_{n=1}^{2}q^{0}_{n}\delta^{(3)}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{r}_{n}(t))\,,

(643)

The equation of motion of the particles (
640
) simplifies to

\displaystyle u_{n}^{\;\;\alpha}\nabla_{\alpha}u_{n}^{\;\;\beta}=-\frac{q^{0}_{j}}{m_{j}+q^{0}_{j}\Phi_{0}}g^{\alpha\beta}\Phi_{,\alpha}\,.

(644)

We see that the particles are accelerated by the scalar. With the Newtonian metric (
642
) and assuming
q_{1},q_{2}\ll|\boldsymbol{r}_{2}-\boldsymbol{r}_{1}|
, this equation can be written in a familiar form
61
61

61

One can see this directly by plugging the Newtonian metric (
642
) inside the particles action (
14
) obtaining

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\textrm{part}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}m_{j}\int dt\sqrt{(1+2U)-|d\boldsymbol{r}_{j}/dt|^{2}}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{j}m_{j}\int dt\left(1+U-\tfrac{1}{2}|d\boldsymbol{r}_{j}/dt|^{2}\right)\,.

(645)

This is just the action describing a non-relativistic system of particles in a gravitational potential
U
.

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\boldsymbol{r}_{n}=-\boldsymbol{\nabla}U(t,\boldsymbol{r}_{n})-\frac{q^{0}_{n}}{m_{n}}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\Phi_{0}(t,\boldsymbol{r}_{n})\,,

(646)

where
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
is the usual
3
-dimensional gradient operator.
Using equation (
641
) we obtain
62
62

62

Actually, in this step we cannot really consider point sources, otherwise we would find problems with a diverging “self-force”. Fortunately, this is not a real problem, and we can proceed by assuming that the particles have a small, but finite size.

\displaystyle U(t,\boldsymbol{r}_{1})=\frac{Gm_{2}}{|\boldsymbol{r}_{2}(t)-\boldsymbol{r}_{1}|}\,,\quad

\displaystyle U(t,\boldsymbol{r}_{2})=\frac{Gm_{1}}{|\boldsymbol{r}_{2}-\boldsymbol{r}_{1}(t)|}\,,

(647)

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}(t,\boldsymbol{r}_{1})=\frac{q_{2}^{0}}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{r}_{2}(t)-\boldsymbol{r}_{1}|}\,,\quad

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}(t,\boldsymbol{r}_{2})=\frac{q_{1}^{0}}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{r}_{2}-\boldsymbol{r}_{1}(t)|}\,.

(648)

15..1.3
Elliptic motion and orbit-averaging

As one expects, Eqs. (
646
) with (
648
) describes a Keplerian orbital motion with energy and angular momentum given by Eq. (
380
).
These differ from (
368
) and (
369
) due to the scalar interaction. Using spherical coordinates with origin at the system’s center of mass the trajectories can be written as
\boldsymbol{r}_{1}=\left(r_{1}(\varphi_{p}),\varphi_{p},\pi/2\right)
and
\boldsymbol{r}_{2}=\left(r_{2}(\varphi_{p}),\varphi_{p}+\pi,\pi/2\right)
with

\displaystyle r_{1}=\frac{m_{2}}{M}r_{p}\,,\qquad r_{2}=\frac{m_{1}}{M}r_{p}\,,

(649)

\displaystyle r_{p}(\varphi_{p})=\frac{a(1-e^{2})}{1+e\cos\varphi_{p}}\,.

(650)

Their angular velocity is

\displaystyle\dot{\varphi}_{p}=\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{G}M}{a^{3}}}(1-e^{2})^{-3/2}(1+e\cos\varphi)^{2}\,.

(651)

Finally, we define the average of a quantity
X
over one period
T
as

\left<X\right>=\frac{\omega_{0}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{d\varphi}{\dot{\varphi}}X(\varphi)\,.

(652)

where
\omega_{0}
is the (Keplerian) orbital frequency.

15..1.4
Radiation emitted by a Newtonian binary

A Newtonian binary sources a scalar field described by Eq. (
639
), which can be put in the form

\displaystyle\Box\Phi=\rho(t,\boldsymbol{x})\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\sum_{n=1}^{2}q^{0}_{n}\delta^{(3)}\left(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{r}_{n}(t)\right)\,.

(653)

Thus, the binary will lose energy and angular momentum through this channel and the motion will not be truly Keplerian; the radiation reaction force entering (
640
) (which we are neglecting in the computation of the radiation, since we are using an adiabatic approximation) will be responsible for a deviation to the Keplerian orbit. Let us compute the radiation emitted by this binary of scalar charges at the (leading) dipole approximation.

In the Newtonian approximation the scalar radiation propagates in flat space. So, the solution of the (sourced) scalar wave equation is

\displaystyle\Phi(t,\boldsymbol{x})=\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\sqrt{-g^{\prime}}\,\frac{\rho(t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}|,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}|}\,.

(654)

In the dipole approximation it is straightforward to show that

\displaystyle\Phi(t,r\to\infty,\theta,\varphi)\simeq\frac{1}{4\pi r}\boldsymbol{e}_{r}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{p}}(t-r)\,,

(655)

with the dipole moment

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}(t)\equiv\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\sqrt{-g^{\prime}}\rho(t,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}=\left(\frac{q^{0}_{1}m_{2}-q^{0}_{2}m_{1}}{M}\right)\boldsymbol{r}_{p}(t)\,.

This approximation is valid for scalar waves with frequency
\omega\sim\omega_{0}\ll 1/a
, where
\omega_{0}
is the orbital frequency (which is compatible with the Newtonian approximation).
The radiated energy flux is

\displaystyle\dot{E}^{\textrm{rad}}=-\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{2}\int d\Omega\,T^{S}_{rt}\,,

(656)

and the angular momentum through

\displaystyle\dot{L}^{\textrm{rad}}=\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{2}\int d\Omega\,T^{S}_{r\varphi}\,.

(657)

Plugging the dipole approximation in the (real) scalar’s energy-momentum tensor (
10
) we can write the last two expressions in the form

\displaystyle\dot{E}^{\textrm{rad}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{q^{0}_{1}m_{2}-q^{0}_{2}m_{1}}{4\pi M}\right)^{2}\int d\Omega\,\left[\boldsymbol{e}_{r}\cdot\ddot{\boldsymbol{r}}_{p}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{12\pi}\frac{\tilde{G}^{2}}{r_{p}^{4}}(q^{0}_{1}m_{2}-q^{0}_{2}m_{1})^{2}\,,

(658)

where we used
\ddot{\boldsymbol{r}}_{p}=-\tilde{G}M\boldsymbol{r}_{p}/r_{p}^{3}
and integrated over the sphere, and

\displaystyle\dot{L}^{\textrm{rad}}=-\left(\frac{q^{0}_{1}m_{2}-q^{0}_{2}m_{1}}{4\pi M}\right)^{2}\int d\Omega\,\left(\boldsymbol{e}_{r}\cdot\ddot{\boldsymbol{r}}_{p}\right)\partial_{\varphi}\left(\boldsymbol{e}_{r}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{r}}_{p}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{12\pi}\tilde{G}^{\frac{3}{2}}\frac{\sqrt{a(1-e^{2})}}{\sqrt{M}r_{p}^{3}}(q^{0}_{1}m_{2}-q^{0}_{2}m_{1})^{2}\,.

(659)

Averaging over an orbit we find

\displaystyle\langle\dot{E}^{\textrm{rad}}\rangle=\frac{1}{24\pi}\frac{\tilde{G}^{2}}{a^{4}}(q^{0}_{1}m_{2}-q^{0}_{2}m_{1})^{2}\left(\frac{2+e^{2}}{(1-e^{2})^{\frac{5}{2}}}\right)\,,

(660)

\displaystyle\langle\dot{L}^{\textrm{rad}}\rangle=\frac{1}{12\pi}\frac{\tilde{G}^{\frac{3}{2}}}{\sqrt{M}a^{\frac{5}{2}}(1-e^{2})}(q^{0}_{1}m_{2}-q^{0}_{2}m_{1})^{2}\,,

(661)

resulting in the ratio

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\dot{L}^{\textrm{rad}}\rangle}{\langle\dot{E}^{\textrm{rad}}\rangle}=\frac{\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}{\omega_{0}}\left(\frac{1-e^{2}}{1+\frac{e^{2}}{2}}\right)\,.

(662)

In the adiabatic approximation the major semi-axis and the eccentricity follow

\displaystyle\langle\dot{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{2a^{2}\langle\dot{E}^{\textrm{rad}}\rangle}{\tilde{G}m_{1}m_{2}}<0\,,

(663)

\displaystyle\langle\dot{e}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{M}{\tilde{G}a}}\frac{\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}{e}\frac{\langle\dot{E}^{\textrm{rad}}\rangle}{m_{1}m_{2}}\left(\frac{\langle\dot{L}^{\textrm{rad}}\rangle}{\langle\dot{E}^{\textrm{rad}}\rangle}-\frac{\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}{\omega_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\frac{M}{\tilde{G}a}}\left(\frac{1-e^{2}}{e\,\omega_{0}}\right)\frac{\langle\dot{E}^{\textrm{rad}}\rangle}{m_{1}m_{2}}\left(\frac{3e^{2}}{2+e^{2}}\right)\leq 0\,.

(664)

Thus, the emission of scalar radiation by a binary causes the major semi-axis and the eccentricity to decrease in time: the orbit shrinks and circularizes.
In the circular orbit limit our results are in agreement with those of Refs.
[
222
,
223
,
224
]
.

15..2
Vector charged

15..2.1
Theory

Here we consider the theory (
2
) with currents

\displaystyle J_{S}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(665)

\displaystyle J_{V}^{\;\;\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{2}q_{n}^{1}\int d\tau_{n}\,\frac{dx_{n}^{\;\;\alpha}}{d\tau_{n}}\frac{\delta^{(4)}\left(x^{\delta}-x_{n}^{\;\;\delta}(\tau_{n})\right)}{\sqrt{-g}}\,.

(666)

Particle
n
has mass
m_{n}
and electric charge
q^{1}_{n}
.

The sourced Maxwell
EOM
(
6
)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}F^{\alpha\beta}=J_{V}^{\;\;\beta}\,.

(667)

In the Newtonian approximation and neglecting radiation (valid for slowly moving charges) we can repeat the exact same steps that we applied to the scalar charges to find that the electric charges also describe a Keplerian orbit; the only difference being that in the definition of
\tilde{G}
we have now electric charges instead of scalar charges.
The energy-momentum tensor of the electromagnetic field is given in Eq. (
11
).

15..2.2
Radiation emitted by a Newtonian binary

Again, the binary will radiate energy and angular momentum – in this case through electromagnetic waves – and the motion will not be truly Keplerian; we are considering the regime in which the orbits change adiabatically.

Using the Lorenz gauge
\nabla_{\mu}A^{\mu}=0
the sourced Maxwell equations become

\displaystyle\Box A^{\alpha}=J_{V}^{\;\;\alpha}\,,

(668)

which we can decompose into

\displaystyle\Box\Phi=\rho(t,\boldsymbol{x})\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\sum_{n=1}^{2}q^{1}_{n}\delta^{(3)}\left(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{r}_{n}\right)\,,

(669)

\displaystyle\Box\boldsymbol{A}=\boldsymbol{j}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\sum_{n=1}^{2}q^{1}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\delta^{(3)}\left(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{r}_{n}\right)\,,

(670)

where we used that the sources are non-relativistic. In the Newtonian approximation we consider that the electromagnetic waves propagate in flat space. So, the solution to the (sourced) Maxwell equations is

\displaystyle\Phi(t,\boldsymbol{x})=\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\sqrt{-g^{\prime}}\,\frac{\rho(t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}|,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}|}\,,

(671)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x})=\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\sqrt{-g^{\prime}}\,\frac{\boldsymbol{j}(t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}|,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}|}\,.

(672)

In the dipole approximation one can show that

\displaystyle\Phi(t,r\to\infty,\theta,\varphi)\simeq\frac{1}{4\pi r}\boldsymbol{e}_{r}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{p}}(t-r)\,,

(673)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}(t,r\to\infty,\theta,\varphi)\simeq\frac{1}{4\pi r}\dot{\boldsymbol{p}}(t-r)\,,

(674)

with the dipole moment

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}(t)\equiv\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\sqrt{-g^{\prime}}\rho(t,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}=\left(\frac{q^{1}_{1}m_{2}-q^{1}_{2}m_{1}}{M}\right)\boldsymbol{r}_{p}(t)\,.

Now, the magnetic field is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}(t,r\to\infty,\theta,\varphi)\equiv\boldsymbol{\nabla}\times\boldsymbol{A}\simeq-\frac{1}{4\pi r}\boldsymbol{e}_{r}\times\ddot{\boldsymbol{p}}(t-r)

(675)

and using Ampère-Maxwell’s law we have

\displaystyle\dot{\boldsymbol{E}}(t,r\to\infty,\theta,\varphi)=\boldsymbol{\nabla}\times\boldsymbol{B}=\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}\times\boldsymbol{e}_{r}\,,

(676)

which, integrating in time, gives the electric field

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}(t,r\to\infty,\theta,\varphi)=\boldsymbol{B}\times\boldsymbol{e}_{r}\,.

(677)

These result in the Poynting vector

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{S}(t,r\to\infty,\theta,\varphi)\equiv\boldsymbol{E}\times\boldsymbol{B}=|\boldsymbol{B}|^{2}\boldsymbol{e}_{r}\,,

(678)

where we used Lagrange’s rule for the triple cross product and that
(\boldsymbol{B}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{r})=0
.
Now using the scalar quadruple product identity we have

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{B}|^{2}=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}r^{2}}\left(|\ddot{\boldsymbol{p}}|^{2}-\left(\ddot{\boldsymbol{p}}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{r}\right)^{2}\right)\,.

(679)

So the radiated energy flux is

\displaystyle\dot{E}^{\textrm{rad}}

\displaystyle=-\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{2}\int d\Omega\,T^{V}_{rt}=\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{2}\int d\Omega\,\boldsymbol{S}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{r}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{q^{1}_{1}m_{2}-q^{1}_{2}m_{1}}{4\pi M}\right)^{2}\int d\Omega\,\left[|\ddot{\boldsymbol{r}}_{p}|^{2}-\left(\ddot{\boldsymbol{r}}_{p}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{r}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6\pi}\frac{\tilde{G}^{2}}{r_{p}^{4}}(q^{1}_{1}m_{2}-q^{1}_{2}m_{1})^{2}\,,

(680)

where we used
\ddot{\boldsymbol{r}}_{p}=-\tilde{G}M\boldsymbol{e}_{r}/r^{2}
and integrated over the sphere.
The radiated angular momentum flux

\displaystyle\dot{L}^{\textrm{rad}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{2}\int d\Omega\,T^{V}_{r\varphi}

\displaystyle=2\left(\frac{q^{1}_{1}m_{2}-q^{1}_{2}m_{1}}{4\pi M}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\times\int d\Omega\,\left[\left(\boldsymbol{e}_{r}\cdot\ddot{\boldsymbol{r}}_{p}\right)\partial_{\varphi}\left(\boldsymbol{e}_{r}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{r}}_{p}\right)-\left(\boldsymbol{e}_{r}\cdot\ddot{\boldsymbol{r}}_{p}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{e}_{\varphi}\cdot\ddot{\boldsymbol{r}}_{p}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6\pi}\tilde{G}^{\frac{3}{2}}\frac{\sqrt{a(1-e^{2})}}{\sqrt{M}r_{p}^{3}}(q^{1}_{1}m_{2}-q^{1}_{2}m_{1})^{2}\,.

(681)

Thus, averaging over one orbital period, we conclude that electric charges radiate twice the energy and twice the angular momentum per unit of time in comparison with scalar charges (compare with Eqs. (
658
) and (
659
)). So, the ratio between the angular momentum and energy carried by the radiated electromagnetic field
\langle\dot{L}^{\textrm{rad}}\rangle/\langle\dot{E}^{\textrm{rad}}\rangle
is the same as for the scalar field and is given by (
662
). So, the emission of electromagnetic waves by a binary causes both the major semi-axis and eccentricity to decrease in time: the orbit shrinks and circularizes (see (
663
) and (
664
)).
Our results for the electromagnetic radiation emitted by a binary are in agreement with the ones of Refs.
[
225
,
226
]
.

†
†
margin:

16

Generalized Lorentz law

To obtain equation (
433
), we observe that the charged test fields (with charge current density
J^{\alpha}
) generate an extra electromagnetic field with Faraday tensor
f
satisfying the Maxwell equations
df=0
and

\nabla_{\mu}f^{\mu\nu}=-J^{\nu}\,.

(682)

The total electromagnetic energy-momentum tensor is then

T^{EM}_{\mu\nu}=(F_{\mu\alpha}+f_{\mu\alpha})(F_{\nu}^{\,\,\,\,\alpha}+f_{\nu}^{\,\,\,\,\alpha})-\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}(F_{\alpha\beta}+f_{\alpha\beta})(F^{\alpha\beta}+f^{\alpha\beta})\,.

(683)

Besides the stationary part, due solely to
F
, one has to consider, in the test field approximation, the cross terms

t_{\mu\nu}=f_{\mu\alpha}F_{\nu}^{\,\,\,\,\alpha}+F_{\mu\alpha}f_{\nu}^{\,\,\,\,\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}F_{\alpha\beta}f^{\alpha\beta}\,.

(684)

We have

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\nabla^{\mu}t_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-J_{\alpha}F_{\nu}^{\,\,\,\,\alpha}+f_{\mu\alpha}\nabla^{\mu}F_{\nu}^{\,\,\,\,\alpha}+F_{\mu\alpha}\nabla^{\mu}f_{\nu}^{\,\,\,\,\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\nu}F_{\alpha\beta})f^{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}F_{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{\nu}f^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-F_{\nu\alpha}J^{\alpha},

(685)

where we used (
682
), the Maxwell equation
\nabla^{\mu}F_{\mu\alpha}=0
, the fact that

f_{\mu\alpha}\nabla^{\mu}F^{\nu\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}(\nabla^{\nu}F_{\alpha\beta})f^{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla^{\alpha}F^{\nu\beta}+\nabla^{\beta}F^{\alpha\nu}-\nabla^{\nu}F^{\alpha\beta}\right)=0

(686)

(because of the Maxwell equation
dF=0
), and the fact that

F_{\mu\alpha}\nabla^{\mu}f^{\nu\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}F_{\alpha\beta}\nabla^{\nu}f^{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}F_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla^{\alpha}f^{\nu\beta}+\nabla^{\beta}f^{\alpha\nu}-\nabla^{\nu}f^{\alpha\beta}\right)=0

(687)

(because of the Maxwell equation
df=0
). Therefore, in the test field approximation, we have

\nabla^{\mu}\left(T_{\mu\nu}+T^{EM}_{\mu\nu}\right)=0\Leftrightarrow\nabla^{\mu}\left(T_{\mu\nu}+t_{\mu\nu}\right)=0\Leftrightarrow\nabla^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}=F_{\nu\alpha}J^{\alpha},

(688)

which is equation (
433
).

One may wonder why not use the conserved current

\nabla_{\mu}(T^{\mu\nu}K_{\nu}+t^{\mu\nu}K_{\nu})=0

(689)

to define the energy of the test field as

E^{\prime\prime}=\int_{\mathcal{S}}dV_{3}(T^{\mu\nu}+t^{\mu\nu})K_{\nu}N_{\mu}\,.

(690)

The reason is that this expression accounts for the energy of the interaction between the charged field and the background electromagnetic field through the electromagnetic cross terms (
684
), whereas (
443
) localizes it on the charges. As is well known, the physical mass of a charged
BH
includes the energy of its background electromagnetic field; when charge enters the
BH
, the interaction energy should be transferred from the energy of the electromagnetic field to the
BH
’s mass. This accounting is accomplished by (
443
), but not by (
690
).
63
63

63

As a toy model of this situation, consider a distribution of test charges with density
\rho
on a background electrostatic field
\boldsymbol{E}=-\boldsymbol{\nabla}\phi
generated by a closed surface kept at a constant potential
\Phi
. Using Gauss’s law, it is easily seen that the total electrostatic energy outside the surface is
\int_{\text{out}}\rho\phi=\int_{\text{out}}\boldsymbol{E}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}-q_{\text{in}}\Phi
, where
\boldsymbol{e}
is the electric field generated by the test charges and
q_{\text{in}}
is the total test charge inside the surface.

One might also worry that the presence of the extra energy-momentum tensor
t^{\mu\nu}
with nonzero divergence (
685
) could invalidate our previous conclusions. That is not the case, however, because
t^{\mu\nu}
does not contribute to the flux across the horizon. In fact, using (
438
) and the Killing equation (
434
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{H}t_{\mu\nu}Z^{\mu}Z^{\nu}

\displaystyle=\int_{H}2f_{\mu}^{\,\,\,\,\alpha}F_{\nu\alpha}Z^{\mu}Z^{\nu}=\int_{H}2f_{\mu}^{\,\,\,\,\alpha}(\nabla_{\nu}A_{\alpha}-\nabla_{\alpha}A_{\nu})Z^{\mu}Z^{\nu}

\displaystyle=\int_{H}2f_{\mu}^{\,\,\,\,\alpha}(A^{\nu}\nabla_{\nu}Z_{\alpha}-Z^{\nu}\nabla_{\alpha}A_{\nu})Z^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\int_{H}2f_{\mu}^{\,\,\,\,\alpha}(-A^{\nu}\nabla_{\alpha}Z_{\nu}-Z^{\nu}\nabla_{\alpha}A_{\nu})Z^{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\int_{H}2Z^{\mu}f_{\mu}^{\,\,\,\,\alpha}\nabla_{\alpha}(A^{\nu}Z_{\nu})=0\,,

(691)

since the vector field
Z^{\mu}f_{\mu}^{\,\,\,\,\alpha}
is tangent to the event horizon, i. e.,

Z^{\mu}f_{\mu}^{\,\,\,\,\alpha}Z_{\alpha}=0,

(692)

and
A^{\nu}Z_{\nu}=\Phi
is constant along the event horizon.

Mathematica

MaTex
